金融市場投資週報                 2026 年 9 月 21 日
上週重點訊息
◼ Fed 三年來首次升息，點陣圖顯示年底前再升一碼：Fed 於 9/16 一致決議升息 1 碼，將聯
邦基金利率目標區間提高至 3.75%～4.00%，為 2023 年 7 月以來首次升息。最新利率預測
中，18 名官員有 16 人預期年底前至少仍需再升息 1 碼。最新經濟預測顯示，Fed 上修今明
兩年實質 GDP 成長率並下修失業率預估值，但也預期通膨壓力將比原先估計更為頑強。主席
Warsh 並指出美國經濟、就業、內需與資本投資仍具韌性，金融環境難稱緊縮。

◼ AI 安全疑慮引發半導體股急跌，但科技產業內部仍呈現明顯分化： OpenAI、Anthropic 等
AI 業者高層呼籲放慢 AI 發展速度、強化安全防護，引發市場重新評估 AI 投資成長速度與資
本支出前景，拖累費城半導體指數 9/14 重挫約 5.9%，但軟體股反而上漲。現階段尚不足以
證明 AI 資本支出循環已反轉，資金亦未全面撤離科技產業，而是從估值較高、籌碼較擁擠的
AI 硬體轉向其他科技次產業。
◼ 日本央行決議升息 1 碼，符合市場預期：日本央行 9/18 以 7 比 2 決議將無擔保隔夜拆款利
率由 1.0%調升至 1.25%。此為該行 2024 年來第 6 次升息，符合市場預期，故升息後對風險
資產衝擊有限。BOJ 明言將「繼續升息並調整寬鬆程度」 ，並將審視調整的時點與步調。
市場展望與操作策略
◼ 科技股中期趨勢未見反轉，但短線波動料加劇：儘管 FOMC 9 月升息，點陣圖暗示今年可能
再升一碼，但其對經濟成長率與失業率預期仍朝正向發展，同時股市也止跌上揚，顯示投資

人認為企業獲利成長動能足以抵消利率上揚的負面影響，基本面較佳的科技股仍具資金承
接，惟宜留意股市短線仍可能因地緣政治與經濟數據表現而出現波動。
◼ 中天期美債及投資級債風險報酬相對均衡：Fed 重啟升息且點陣圖暗示年底前或再升息，短天
期公債殖利率直接受到政策利率上修影響 ； 天期過長的債券則持續面臨財政赤字 、 發債供給及
期限溢價上升壓力，利率敏感度偏高。中天期(5~10 年期)美國公債既可取得目前相對較高收
益，亦具適度存續期間，在未來通膨降溫時較有機會受惠於殖利率回落，風險報酬較為均衡。
投資級債亦具較高收益與信用防禦特性，有助平衡投資組合波動。

通膨升溫壓抑美股表現，近期聚焦財報結果
2026 年 10 月 15 日
一、 市場動態
1. 科技股領跌，費半連 6 日收黑：美股主要指數 10 月 14 日全數下跌，道瓊工業指數下
跌 0.09%，收 51,621.48 點；S&P 500 指數下跌 1.11%，收 7,718.94 點，為 7 月 29
日以來最大單日跌幅；Nasdaq 綜合指數下跌 1.88%，收 26,692.87 點，為 7 月 23
日以來最大單日跌幅。費城半導體指數下跌 4.30%，收 11,684.38 點，連續第６個交
易日走低，為 2025 年 5 月以來最長連跌紀錄。

2. 類股非全面下挫，資金仍呈輪動現象：半導體及 AI 相關股票在 10/14 跌幅尤其明顯
，AMD 下跌 5.73%、Nvidia 下跌 3.16%、Broadcom 下跌 3.10%，AI 基礎建設相關
的 CoreWeave、Nebius、Oracle 及 Dell 跌幅更深，顯示市場對資本支出規模較大、
融資成本敏感度較高的 AI 相關企業風險偏好明顯下降。不過，S&P 500 的 11 大類
股中有 8 類下跌，資訊科技及通訊服務為主要拖累來源，但醫療保健、必需消費與能
源類股仍逆勢上漲。
3. 美國 10 年期公債殖利率盤中急升後回落：美國 10 年期公債殖利率 10/14 由 5.2%左
右快速升至約 5.33%，逼近近期高點；然而，隨後市場消化 Fed 官員較審慎的政策訊
號，殖利率自高檔回落，終場約收於 5.19%，較前一交易日 5.21%小幅下降約 2 個基
點。
二、 下跌原因分析
1. 美 9 月通膨高於預期 ，Fed 再升息風險重新升高 ：美國 10/14 公布 9 月 CPI 月增 0.6%

、年增率也由 8 月的 3.4%上揚至 3.7%；核心 CPI 月增 0.3%、年增率由 8 月的 2.4%
升至 2.6%。其中汽油價格單月大漲 9.1%，貢獻整體 CPI 月增幅超過一半；住宅成本
亦月增 0.4%，顯示物價壓力仍具黏性。此數據公布後，市場重新提高 Fed 進一步升
息的預期 ，CME FedWatch 所反映的 10 月升息 1 碼機率一度由前一日約 22%提高至
37%。雖然截至 10/15 台北時間早上 7 點左右該機率回降至 29.3%，這也提醒投資人
Fed 的升息路徑仍存在不確定性。
2. 高利率同時壓縮科技股估值與提高 AI 融資成本 ：科技及半導體公司未來獲利成長占評
價的比重較高，因此對折現率變化相對敏感。當長天期美債殖利率維持 5%以上，市
場願意支付的本益比自然受到限制，即使企業獲利仍維持成長，股價也可能因評價倍
數下降而出現修正。另一方面，本輪 AI 投資需要大量資料中心、伺服器、電力與網路
基礎設施，高利率亦直接增加企業融資成本，反映信用市場對高度依賴外部資金的 AI

基礎建設公司要求更高風險溢酬。

策，應審慎衡量本身之需求及投資風險，自行承擔一切投資風險並自負盈虧，本行不負任何法律責任。本報告受中華民國著作權法保護為元
大銀行所有，非經本行同意，不得將本報告內容之一部或全部加以複製、轉載或散布。本報告所載述的意見本行可隨時予以更改或撤回，恕
不另行通知。
3. AI 投資焦點由「支出規模」轉向「投資報酬率」:近期市場對 AI 的疑慮逐漸由「需求
是否成長」 ，轉向「龐大的資本支出能否轉化為足夠營收、獲利與自由現金流」 。
Broadcom 執行長陳福陽近期亦指出 ，AI 未來發展的重要限制可能逐漸由晶片或記憶
體供應轉向資本本身。我們認為，這並不代表AI 資本支出週期即將結束，而是市場已
由第一階段的 「資本支出愈大、成長想像愈高」 ，逐步進入更重視資本效率與投資報酬
率的第二階段。未來財務結構、現金流與AI 商業化能力，將成為不同企業評價分化的
重要因素。
4. 中國記憶體競爭疑慮加重半導體短線壓力：中國長鑫存儲（CXMT）10/10 傳出記憶

體技術取得進展 ， 引發市場對未來DRAM 供給增加及價格競爭的疑慮 ， 記憶體相關類
股率先承壓，並進一步影響整體半導體市場情緒。惟 DRAM 與 GPU、客製化 ASIC、
先進晶圓製程及先進封裝的產業競爭與需求結構並不相同。我們認為，儘管記憶體競
爭升溫值得關注，但目前尚不足以延伸為整體 AI 半導體需求同步轉弱。
5. 高檔籌碼加速獲利了結：AI 與半導體仍是 2026 年美股表現最強勢的投資主題之一，
部分科技股今年累計漲幅已相當可觀。當通膨、殖利率及AI 投資報酬疑慮同時升高，
先前漲幅較大、投資人部位較集中的族群自然成為優先調節對象。因此，本波費半快
速修正除了總經與基本面疑慮外，亦包含籌碼降溫及風險部位收縮因素，使短期價格
波動幅度可能高於企業基本面實際變化。
三、市場看法
1. 類股輪動明顯，尚非全面看空美股：科技與 AI 相關股票 10 月 14 日明顯承壓，但醫
療保健、必需消費與能源類股仍逆勢上漲，道瓊工業指數僅下跌 0.09%。此外，S&P
500 成分股下跌家數與上漲家數之比約為 1.1：1；相較於指數下跌 1.11%，市場廣度

並未同步大幅惡化。這反映資金主要由高評價且前期漲幅較大的科技與AI 族群，轉向
現金流較穩定的防禦型產業。現階段市場特徵較接近類股輪動與風格切換，而非無差
別的全面風險撤離，亦尚未看到美股企業獲利或經濟基本面全面反轉的明確訊號。
2. 美股科技股財報前風險降載，Q3 仍存在重新定價空間：近期科技股修正，費城半導
體指數連續６個交易日下跌，也可能部分反映第三季財報季正式展開前，投資人在股
價先前漲幅較大、評價偏高及利率環境不確定下，提前降低風險曝險。真正決定後續
市場方向的關鍵 ， 仍在於實際企業獲利能否支持目前的高成長預期 。根據 Bloomberg
季度獲利成長追蹤資料，今年 Q1 與 Q2 財報季期間的 EPS 成長率經驗顯示，隨實際
財報陸續公布，企業獲利成長曾明顯優於財報季開始前的市場預期。儘管這並不保證
Q3 將複製前兩季的上修幅度，但目前市場因財報不確定性所反映的風險折價仍存在
被實際財報重新驗證的可能。若大型科技及半導體公司後續營收、獲利與AI 需求指引
實際公布結果優於預期，近期風險溢價可望部分收斂；反之，若獲利成長無法跟上目

前評價，市場整理時間則可能進一步延長。

不另行通知。
3. 費半修正幅度大，但 AI 需求尚未出現全面反轉：ASML 在 10/14 所公布的最新第三
季財報反而顯示 AI 相關設備需求仍相當強勁，該公司表示 2027 與 2028 年規劃產能
可能仍無法完全滿足客戶需求，部分客戶亦已表達對 2029 及 2030 年設備需求的興
趣。儘管費城半導體指數近期市場情緒轉弱，但由 ASML 的財報表現來看，目前仍未
看到大型 AI 客戶全面取消晶片訂單、主要雲端服務商同步削減 AI 資本支出，或先進
製程需求快速降溫等結構性訊號。
4. 台積電營收顯示 AI 需求暢旺 ， 留意10/15 法說會內容：台積電 10/8 公布的第三季營

收達新台幣 1.49 兆元、年增約 50%，創歷史新高，並高於市場原先預估的 1.46 兆元
；9 月單月營收年增 54.6%。強勁的營收表現仍反映 AI 與先進製程需求具有支撐。目
前市場因此呈現「企業營收仍快速成長，但股票評價下降」的狀況。高殖利率環境下
，EPS 上升與本益比下降可以同時發生，後續股價方向將取決於企業獲利上修幅度是
否足以抵銷評價收縮。該公司將於 10/15 下午舉行第三季法人說明會。市場重點將集
中於第四季展望、AI 與 HPC 需求、先進製程、先進封裝及未來資本支出規劃。公司
對 AI 需求與產能投資的最新判斷，將成為判斷近期費半修正主要屬於評價調整，或產
業基本面是否開始出現變化的重要依據。
5. AI 與半導體進入基本面分化階段：我們認為，在高利率環境下，高度仰賴外部融資、
自由現金流尚未成熟的 AI 基礎建設業者 ， 受到的資金成本壓力明顯高於具穩定現金流
的大型科技與雲端服務商。同樣地，記憶體、GPU、ASIC、先進製程及先進封裝各有
不同供需結構。我們預期下一階段 AI 行情將更加重視訂單能見度、產品競爭力、毛利

率、自由現金流及 AI 營收實際兌現，產業內部強弱分化程度可能進一步提高。
6. Fed 路徑仍存不確定性，惟企業獲利可望提供股市支撐：美國 9 月 CPI 升溫使市場重
新評估 Fed 後續升息時點與幅度，美國利率政策仍存在不確定性，長天期美債殖利率
也可能維持高檔。不過，現階段企業獲利成長與AI 相關需求仍具韌性，若第三季財報

延續前兩季優於預期的趨勢，企業 EPS 若能上修有望部分抵銷高利率對本益比的壓縮
效果。換言之，後續市場表現未必只由 Fed 政策主導，企業實際獲利能否持續兌現，
將是支撐科技與半導體股中期走勢的重要力量。
四、投資建議
1. 短線聚焦美股財報與 Fed 決策，科技股中期趨勢料不致有變：近期市場同時面臨財報
前的觀望態勢、美債殖利率偏高及 AI 資本支出疑慮等不確定因素，短線風險偏好可能
受到壓抑，後續宜關注 10 月美股財報對企業獲利與資本支出展望，以及 FOMC 會議
所釋出的政策訊號。中期而言，目前AI 相關企業營運與資本支出尚未出現全面轉弱跡

象，科技產業基本面仍具一定支撐，但在利率維持高檔及市場評價偏高的環境下，後
續宜更加重視企業獲利、現金流及資本支出的實際變化。
2. 中天期債券風險報酬仍較均衡 ：美國 9 月 CPI 升溫使短端殖利率持續受到 Fed 政策路
徑影響，而 20～30 年超長端仍面臨財政赤字、國債供給與期限溢價偏高等結構性壓
力。相較之下，目前殖利率已位於歷史相對高檔，若後續核心通膨或能源價格改善，
中段殖利率具有回落條件，同時其存續期間風險又低於超長端，因此現階段以中天期
美國公債及投資級債的風險報酬結構相對較佳。

2330 台積電(TSMC)

報告日期： 2026/10/16 研 究 員：羅 金 鳳 (Sunny Lo ) 
壹、投 資 建 議 
重點摘要：  
⚫ 4Q26營收展望符合預期，但毛利率受 N2良率調整問題而未有
顯著改善，不過因 N2 的出貨量快速大幅成長，有利未來整體
毛利率的改善。  
⚫ 積極擴充產能， N3將擴充三個新廠，且 N2的產能在 2027年
將擴充一倍，而後段的 Interposer 則與 Global  Foundry 進行
合作。  
⚫ AI五年的 CAGR >55%，不含 Agentic AI 所驅動的 CPU強勁
需求，顯見 AI的需求遠優於預期。  
⚫ 我們將 2026/2027 年的稅後 EPS由 106.63/142.76 元，上修
至 111.36/152.74 元。

一. 4Q26營收展望符合預期，但毛利率仍受 N2影響  
3Q26 AI因 AI持續帶動先進製程需求強勁， N3營收比重由 2Q26
的 30%上升至 33%，N2營收比重也由 2Q26 的 3%上升至 8%，
帶動台積電 3Q26 營收達 14,942 億元， QoQ +17.62 %，
YoY +50.95 %，在成本改善、高產能利用率 及製程的升級帶動下，
抵銷 2nm量產初期對毛利率的負影響下， 3Q26毛利率 66.41%，
達公司展望的上緣， 稅後 EPS 2 9.43元，優於預期。 4Q26在 AI
帶動的先進製程及先進封裝需求持續強勁下，預估 以美元對新台
幣 1:31.8 為基礎計算，預估 營收中 值約 16,949 億元，
QoQ+13.4 3%，YoY+ 62.02 %，符合市場預期，惟 仍受 N2量產良
率調整問題的影響，預估 4Q26 整體毛利率維持在 66%左右，，
單季稅後 EPS 32.81元，續創新高 。

二. AI需求強勁，將擴大投資及合作  
由於客戶及客戶的客戶持續釋出 強勁的需求，AI 需求 5 年(2025
～2030)的 CAGR將超越 55%，且此一數字不含 Agentic AI 所帶
動的 CPU需求，主因 CPU主要的應用除了 AI之外，亦應用於傳
統的 PC及非 AI領域，顯見 AI需求的延續性拉長且需求優於預
期，台積電將積極擴產滿足客戶需求，雖然 2026年資本支出維持
600～640億美元，但因持續的海內外擴產加上 2030 年在成本合
理及正面效益下， 會開始導入 Hi-NA EUV，因此，預估未來數年
的資本支出將大幅走高。

AI帶動 GPU、ASIC需求持續成長，但進入 Agentic AI 則是帶動
CPU需求強勁成長，驅動半導體榮景延續。由於目前需求遠遠大
過台積電所能供應的產能，且持續朝先進製程應用，因此，台積電
目前 N2已在新竹、高雄量產，且 2027年產能將會擴增一倍，應
用於智慧型手機、 HPC、AI，且 A14 將會在 2027 年進行風險性
試產，2028年量產； N3方面也會擴增三個新廠，台南廠的進度符
合預期， 1H27可量產，亞利桑那州第二個 Fab(N3)在 3Q27也可
開始量產 (比預期提前 )，熊本廠於 2028年裝機量產。 由於台積電
美國大客戶及美國政府 希望台積電在美擴大產能，因此，台積電將
會在亞利桑那州以外的德州擴產，會由台積電自已營運 Fab廠，
不過目前尚未有正式結論。 雖然看似擴充相當大的產能，但台積電
都已由上而上的評估且有紀律的擴產，在電力等關鍵指標方面也
都已考慮進去了。

除了先進製程需求強勁外，先進封裝的需求也相對緊俏，因此，台
積電在後段將與 Global Foundry 合作，由 Global Foundry 進行
Interposer 的生產，以滿足客戶需求 ，台積電則是衝刺利潤率更好
的先進製程。

三. 上修 2026/2027年稅後 EPS至 111.36 /152.74元 
2026年受惠 AI及 Agentic AI 需求超乎  預期的帶動下，台積電將
2026年全年的營收由 YoY+40%上修至 44%(以美元計價 )，因此，
我們預估台積電 2026 年營收 55,936 億元，YoY+46.85%，毛利
率 66.55%，稅後淨利 28,877 億元，YoY+68.1%，稅後 EPS由
106.63 元上修至 111.36元。2027年在 GPU、ASIC、CPU、XPU
需求持續強勁的帶動下， 台積電營運仍會是強勁成長的一年，我們
預估台積電 2027 年營收 77,218 億元， YoY+37.17%，毛利率
67.53%，稅後 EPS由 142.76 元上修至 152.74 元。

四. 投資建議維持買進，目標價 3055元 
在 AI需求強勁的帶動下， 先進製程產能持續緊俏，台積電除了積
極擴擴 N3產能外， 2027 年 N2的產能將增加一倍 ，在高階產品
應用上將取得高市佔率， 且在毛利率表現方面，因 先進製程領導地
位的穩固吸引更多訂單， 訂價策略的改變， 及良率的改善，帶動毛
利率維持高檔，帶動獲利 持續大幅成長， 因此，投資建議維持買
進，目標價 3055元(20X2027年稅後 EPS)。

3 
一.公司簡介  
台積電(2330)成立於 1987年，為專業的 Fabless 半導體廠商，為
客戶生產的晶片廣泛地被運用在各種終端市場，例如行動裝置、高
效能運算、車用電子與物聯網等。 2019年，台積電及其子公司所
擁有及管理 約當年產能超過 1,200 萬片十二吋晶圓。在台灣設有
三座十二吋超大晶圓廠、四座八吋晶圓廠和一座六吋晶圓廠，並擁
有一家 100%持有之海外子公司 —台積電（南京）有限公司之十二
吋晶圓廠及二家 100%持有之海外子公司 —WaferTech 美國子公
司、台積電 (中國)有限公司之八吋晶圓廠。 台積電於 2011年第四
季開始導入 28nm，且開始加大先進製程技術的投資，於 2H12以
平面電晶體結構為基礎，在 20nm導入加強版的 HKMG 等技術，
並於 2014年進一步量產 16nm FinFET( 鰭式場效電晶體 )，持續推
進摩爾定律，儘管摩爾定律在 3nm以下將面臨物理極限，台積電
透過先進封裝製程延長摩爾定律，為高端產品 /客戶提供最先進的
技術。為因應美國製造優先及半導在在地化製造的需求，台積電於
2021年開始在美國亞利桑那州建廠，預計 興建六座先進製程廠、
2座先進封裝廠、 1個 R&D中心， 4nm已於 4Q24開始生產，預
估 3nm 也將在 2026 年進行量產，並且因為 AI 需求強勁的帶動
下，將加速 2nm及 16A的進廠速度，預估有機會於 2027～2028
年開始陸續量產。

二.營運概況及展望  
1、發展矽光子技術解決資料傳輸速度不足及訊耗損失問題  
10/2022 ChatGPT 推出後，其廣泛的推理能力，與使用者在各領
域以文字內容的方式對答如流，帶給使用者前所未有的體驗與 驚
豔，使用者呈現爆炸 性的成長，推升生成式 AI快速發展，使得 AI 
Server 呈現供不應求的現象 ，其不論在外觀的設計架構抑或 內部
的零組件規格甚至資料傳輸的頻寬及速度均較一般傳輸的 Server
大幅提升，且內部各元件的規格須同步提升才能使 AI Server 的強
大效能發揮最大的效用。

過往 Server 內部資料的傳輸採用銅線進行傳輸， 由於 AI Server
對資料傳輸的頻寬及傳輸速度的需求大幅提升， 銅線傳輸將產生
較大的訊號減損，使資料傳輸的品質不佳， 而以光做為資料傳輸的
解決方案，可解決資料傳輸訊號減損的問題， 因此，銅退光進的解
決方案勢在必行 。

矽光子的技術為銅退光進的最終解決方案， 但礙於技術及良率尚
不成熟，因此， CPO將為先行發展的技術，以 作為逐步達成光通
訊元件與主晶片直接在矽晶圓製程的階段即同時完成製作 的過渡
技術。在矽光子製作的過程中將以光波導取代 傳統銅線的傳輸，由

於光會沿著折射率較大的 物質前進，空氣的折射率為 1、水的折射
率為 1.33、玻璃的折射率為 1.46、二氧化矽的折射率為 1.6、矽
的折射率為 3.5，因此，光波導將選擇從矽晶圓上製作， 使光能進
行最佳的全反射，讓光能在光波導中的傳 送達最大的效率。由於矽
光子的發展為半導體未來的趨勢，大多數晶圓廠已投入研發，台積
電在此一趨勢下亦不缺席， 進行 40nm～90nm製程的矽光子技術
開發，預估 2025 年將利用 65nm 製程完成緊湊型通用光子引擎
(COUPE)的開發，支援可插拔式的連接器， 2026 年整合 CoWoS
封裝，將 CPO 與 Switch 晶片共同整合於同一基板上，完成共同
封裝光學 (CPO)的開發，未來將進一步將 CPO 與 XPU 共同整合
於矽仲介板上 ，實現晶片間更快的傳輸速度及較低的延時性。

圖一、矽光子發展示 意圖

資料來源： imec；宏遠投顧整理

2、透過 2.5D/3D 封裝延續摩爾定律  
矽為半導體重要材料，矽 1.0為平面式電晶 (Planar FET) 體時代，
由於電子遷移速度受到限制，製程節點 /閘極長度走到大約 28nm；
在 28nm以下進入到矽 2.0，為了改善電子元件漏電的問題， Intel
自 22nm 開始將平面世電晶體改成鰭式場效電晶體 (FinFET)，台
積電亦自 16nm開始將製程改為 FinFET，但當製程節點不斷往下
微縮至 5nm、3nm時，FinFET 將面臨摩爾定律的物理極限，即進
入矽 3.0時代，利用 2.5D及 3D封裝的技術可延續摩爾定律，台
積電在 2.5D封裝方面推出 CoWoS，Intel 推出 EMIB。CoWoS 在
晶片及基板之間加入 Si Interposer( 不具導電功能 )做為連接橋樑；
而 Intel 的 EMIB 則利用矽橋 (Silicon Bridge ；具有元件連通距離
短及反應速度快的特性 )做為多晶片之間連接的橋樑。

圖二、2.5D封裝示意圖

資料來源： Intel & 宏遠投顧彙整

在 3D封裝方面，台積電推出 3D Fabric 平台，Intel 推出 Foveros 
3D封裝架構。台積電 3D Fabric，主要為前段製程採用 Wafer on 
Wafer 及 Chip on Wafer 的 SoIC(系統整合單晶片 )方式，將晶片
進行 3D堆疊，然後結合後段先進封裝製程，包括 CoWoS及InFO，
將邏輯晶片及記憶體晶片進行 2.5D及 3D的封裝製程，達到縮小
IC 面積及提高效能的效果。而 Intel 的 Foveros 將不同製程節點
的晶片進行 3D封裝，包括 10nm製程的運算晶片、 22nm製程的
系統 I/O 晶片和 PoP(Package -on-Package)封裝的 DRAM 記憶
體。

長榮 (2603.TT) 
運價上漲推升 3Q26 獲利，長約調漲支撐 2027 年營運

營收走勢圖

研究員/王建智 MICHAELWANG82121188@UNI-
PSG.COM

研究部對於長榮看法轉趨正向，係因：(1) 3Q26 受惠貨櫃航運
旺季及運價回升，平均運價漲幅明顯高於燃油成本增幅， 帶動獲
利顯著成長。(2) 4Q26 雖進入傳統淡季，惟北美農曆春節前備
貨 、歐洲貨量成長及亞洲區域航線旺季提供支撐，預期獲利雖低
於 3Q26，但仍優於 2Q26。(3) 2027 年雖面臨新船交付增加的供
給壓力， 但全球碼頭擴建速度落後船隊成長，加上塞港及紅海繞
航持續消耗有效運力，有助減緩新增供給對運價的衝擊， 北美及
歐洲航線長約運價亦預期高於 2026 年，支撐全年獲利。預估
2026/2027 年稅後 EPS 分別為45.21 元（+42.7%YoY）／27.89 元
（-38.3%YoY）。歷史本淨比區間為 0.4～1.8 倍，以 2027 年底
預估每股淨值 298.36 元為評價基礎，給予0.95 倍本淨比， 目標
價 283 元，將投資評等調升至買進。

重點摘要： 
1. 3Q26 運價漲幅高於燃油成本增幅，帶動獲利顯著成長 
長榮 3Q26 營收 1,467.76 億元，+39.6%QoQ/+51.4%YoY，主要受
惠第三季貨櫃航運傳統旺季， 歐美航線進入零售商備貨高峰， 帶
動貨運需求回升，加上紅海繞航、 全球港口壅塞持續消耗有效運
力，以及航商透過艙位控管調節供給，推升主要航線運價。7、8
月平均運費分別為 1,539 及1,577 美元／TEU， 參考9 月SCFI 月
均 3,657 點，+8.0%MoM， 推估9 月平均運費約 1,703 美元／TEU，
並假設 9 月貨量與 8 月持平，則 3Q26 平均運費約 1,606 美元／
TEU，+44.7%QoQ，貨量276 萬 TEU，-1.8%QoQ，營收成長主要由
運價上漲貢獻。

成本方面 ， 預估每噸耗油成本由2Q26 的 645 美元升至 714 美元 ，
+10.6%QoQ，假設季度耗油量持平，燃油費用增加約22 億元，惟
運價漲幅明顯高於燃油成本增幅 ， 營收增加帶來的獲利貢獻可望
大幅超過成本上升影響。預估 3Q26 稅後淨利 479.69 億元，
+199.2%QoQ/+120.6%YoY，稅後EPS 22.16 元。

### Page 2

請參見末頁重要批露及免責聲明  2

圖一、各月平均運費、每噸耗油成本、貨量

資料來源：長榮，統一投顧整理 
2. 4Q26 進入傳統淡季，惟區域航線需求支撐營運 
第四季為貨櫃航運傳統淡季， 整體需求不如第三季旺季強勁， 惟
根據訂艙及裝載情況 ， 至11 月前貨量仍具支撐 。 北美航線方面 ，
美國主要港口進口貨櫃量維持高檔，零售庫存銷售比低於 1.3，
顯示零售商庫存水位相對偏低，仍有持續補貨需求，預期 11 月
貨量隨淡季趨緩 ，12 月則有農曆春節前備貨需求支撐 。 歐洲航線
方面 ，2026 年貨量成長優於預期 ， 雖運價自7 月後逐步回落 ， 但
紅海地緣政治風險尚未解除， 繞航仍持續消耗有效運力。 亞洲區
域航線第四季進入傳統旺季 ， 受惠中國加一帶動東南亞及南亞原
料、半成品運輸需求成長。此外，全球塞港造成約300 萬至 400
萬 TEU 有效運力損失，占全球總運力約 11%至 12%，搭配航商艙
位控管，有助支撐淡季運價。

預估 4Q26 營收 1,174.21 億元，-20.0%QoQ/+37.0%YoY，稅後淨
利 255.68 億元，-46.7%QoQ/+200.2%YoY，稅後 EPS 11.81 元。

3. 2027 年新船交付壓力增加，港口壅塞限制有效運力供給 
2027~2029 年為全球貨櫃船新船交付高峰，目前全球貨櫃船總運
力約3,400 萬TEU，至2030 年待交付新船運力超過1,400 萬TEU，
主流研調機構(Clarksons、Drewry)預估 2027 年名目運力供給增
速為 7.7~7.9%，供給成長壓力仍是 2027 年主要風險。惟名目運
力增加不代表有效運力等幅成長 ， 過去十年全球碼頭處理能力年
均成長約 2.7%，低於船隊運力年均成長約 5.4%，港口基礎設施
擴張速度落後船隊成長，塞港問題短期難以改善。紅海及蘇伊士
運河恢復通行亦為重要變數， 歐洲航線若恢復通行，單程約節省

### Page 3

請參見末頁重要批露及免責聲明  3

7 天 、 往返約14 天 ， 但船舶集中抵港可能加劇碼頭壅塞 ， 抵銷部
分航程縮短釋出的運力。長榮截至 2030 年尚有 72 艘新船待交
付 ， 合計運力約89 萬 TEU， 並持續投資重要樞紐碼頭 ， 提升靠泊
效率及船隊調度彈性。

4. 2027 年歐美長約運價預期高於 2026 年，支撐全年獲利 
2027 年北美及歐洲航線長約運價預期均高於 2026 年，主要受惠
三項因素 。 首先 ，2026 年第三季貨櫃運價大幅上漲 ， 目前現貨運
價明顯高於前一年度長約議價時的水準 ， 為新年度合約價格調升
奠定基礎 。 其次 ， 全球港口處理能力擴張速度落後船隊運力成長 ，
加上紅海繞航及主要港口壅塞持續消耗有效運力 ， 航商供給調節
能力提升，有利維持長約議價能力。最後，歐美航線貨運需求仍
具支撐 ， 北美零售商庫存水位偏低 ， 歐洲航線貨量成長優於預期 ，
提供長約價格上調條件。歐洲航線新約自 2027 年初開始適用，
北美航線則自 5 月起生效 ， 預期新年度長約運價調升將逐步反映
於營收，並有助減緩現貨運價波動對獲利的影響，支撐 2027 年
營運表現。

5. 預估 2026/2027 年稅後 EPS 45.21/27.89 元，塞港及長約運
價調升支撐獲利高於歷史平均水準 
預估 2026/2027 年營收分別為 4,558.69／4,178.70 億元，分別
+20.3%YoY/-8.3%YoY，稅後淨利分別為 978.74／603.81 億元，
分別+42.7%YoY/-38.3%YoY，稅後 EPS 分別為 45.21 元／27.89
元。2026 年受惠第三季運價大幅上漲 ， 帶動全年獲利回升 。2027
年雖面臨新船交付增加及運價自高檔回落的壓力 ， 惟全球碼頭處
理能力成長落後船隊運力，加上塞港及紅海繞航限制有效供給，
歐美航線長約價格亦預期高於 2026 年 ， 有助減緩獲利下滑幅度 。

Update
Unimicron  | Asia Pacific
Key ABF substrate customer
announces capacity reservation
agreements
What's new? According to  a press release on October 21, at GTC Berlin NVIDIA
(covered by Joseph Moore) named Unimicron as one of  three partners in its plan to
lift substrate capacity 2.5x over three years. A capacity reservation agreement
supports Unimicron’s KF (Kuangfu) plant expansion in Taiwan, with additions
expected from 2Q27 and full capacity by mid-2028.

Our read-through: We believe this implies robust multi-year, AI-driven substrate
demand. NVIDIA’s capacity reservation likely covers Unimicron's 2Q27 KF Plant 2
step flagged in July (see our earnings call takeaways), which could lower the
financial risk of overcapacity as Unimicron deploys its NT$53.7bn 2026 capex
budget. Stay OW with a price target of NT$1,775.

Valuation Methodology and Risks
Unimicron (3037.TW)
Base case, residual income (RI) valuation model, which we think derives the most accurate
value of the firm given that it takes into account cost of equity. We use a cost of equity of 9.2%
[risk-free rate of 1% (10-year Taiwan government note yield), equity risk premium of 8.7%, and
a beta of 1.0], a medium-term growth rate of 14%, and a terminal growth rate of 3%.
Risks to Upside
n Better-than-expected ABF substrate demand from PC and server customers

n Capex cuts; halt to its capacity expansion plan
n Continued yield issues of alternative technology that doesn't require substrate (e.g.,
CoWoP)
Risks to Downside
n Sudden demand shortfall
n Technological change that would not require ABF substrates
n Intensifying competition
n Yield issues or production hiccups when ramping new capacity

STOCK RATINGS
Morgan Stanley uses a relative rating system using terms such as Overweight, Equal-weight, Not-Rated or Underweight (see definitions below). Morgan Stanley does not assign ratings of Buy,
Hold or Sell to the stocks we cover. Overweight, Equal-weight, Not-Rated and Underweight are not the equivalent of buy, hold and sell.  Investors should carefully read the definitions of all
ratings used in Morgan Stanley Research. In addition, since Morgan Stanley Research contains more complete information concerning the analyst's views, investors should carefully read Morgan

Stanley Research, in its entirety, and not infer the contents from the rating alone.  In any case, ratings (or research) should not be used or relied upon as investment advice.  An investor's decision
to buy or sell a stock should depend on individual circumstances (such as the investor's existing holdings) and other considerations.

Analyst Stock Ratings
Overweight (O). The stock's total return is expected to exceed the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next
12-18 months.
Equal-weight (E). The stock's total return is expected to be in line with the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over

the next 12-18 months.
Not-Rated (NR). Currently the analyst does not have adequate conviction about the stock's total return relative to the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage
universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next 12-18 months.
Underweight (U). The stock's total return is expected to be below the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next

12-18 months.
Unless otherwise specified, the time frame for price targets included in Morgan Stanley Research is 12 to 18 months.
Analyst Industry Views
Attractive (A): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be attractive vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated
below.
In-Line (I): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be in line with the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below.

Cautious (C): The analyst views the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months with caution vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below.
Benchmarks for each region are as follows: North America - S&P 500; Latin America - relevant MSCI country index or MSCI Latin America Index; Europe - MSCI Europe; Japan - TOPIX; Asia -
relevant MSCI country index or MSCI sub-regional index or MSCI AC Asia Pacific ex Japan Index.

### Page 1

www.imeritz.com

SK하이닉스 (000660)

Company Brief 
반도체/디스플레이 
Analyst 김선우 
sunwoo.kim@meritz.co.kr 
RA 우서현 
seohyun.woo@meritz.co.kr 3Q26 OP 80.1조원 실적 서프라이즈 예상  
3Q26 영업이익: 상반기와 다르다. 억제 요인들의 추세 반전 전환 
SK하이닉스의 3Q26 영업이익은 전분기 대비 32% 증가한 80.1조원으로 시장기대
치를 능가할 전망이다. 최근 원화강세 흐름 및 상반기 동사의 상대적 실적개선 속도 
열위에 3Q26 영업이익 추정치는 70조원까지 내려갔다 (컨센서스 74조원). 하지만 
이제 동사 출하량 및 판가의 탄력적 복원세가 경쟁사 보다 빠른 실적 개선을 이끌 전
망이다. 3Q26 DRAM과 NAND 출하량/판가 증감률은 각각 +13%/+22%, 
+4%/+15% 수준으로, 각 영업이익은 63조원/17조원으로 예상된다 (OPM 각각 
84%/69% 추정). 그간의 상대적 실적 열위는 이제 반전된다. 이는 1) 판가 전략에 
있어 중장기적 관점에서 고객 중심 유연성 극대화를 꾀했던 부분의 보상적 되돌림이 
발생하고, 2) 업계에서 제일 높은 HBM 판매 비중으로 인해 판가 상승률에 발목 잡혔
지만, 오히려 HBM4 본격 공급이 시작되며 이익기여가 본격화된다. 아울러 3) 모바
일 핵심고객과의 기성 계약이 산업 전반적 LTA 논의로 재검토되며 공급사에 크게 유
리한 조건으로 변화되어 가기 시작한다. 당사는 1Q27부터 모바일 메모리 판가 급등
을 전망하며 (그림1), 2026E/2027E 영업이익의 경우 264조원/454조원으로 상향조정한
다. 4Q26 내 시장 실적 추정치 상향조정 및 투자자 수급의 구조적 개선을 전망한다.

‘지능기술’ 시대, 수요의 가격 역탄력성 속 중장기 기회창출에 주목 
과거 B2C 사이클과 다르게, 금번 B2B 사이클은 수요의 가격 역탄력성을 보이고 있
다. AGI 도달을 위한 선착순 투자가 핵심이기 때문이다. 빅테크 중 낙오자가 발생하
기 전까지 경쟁적 대규모 투자는 지속된다. 최근 동사의 솔리다임 상장 추진보도에 
일부 우려가 제기되고 있지만, 투자자들은 그 이면의 중장기적 기회에 주목해야 한다. 
동사는 LTA 외에도, 다양한 파트너십을 추진하며 중장기 시장 예측력을 증가시키고 
있다. 솔리다임 상장 과정에서 유입되는 현금은 모회사 ‘SK AI co’의 지분교환 및 JV 
구축 등에 활용되리라 예상된다. 이는 중장기 수요를 ‘창출’시키는 개념이다.

순수한 주주가치 제고 목적 환원 재도출 예상. 적정주가 상향조정 
빠르게 증가한 현금흐름 중 ‘잉여’분은 주주가치 ‘회복’에 우선 배분돼야 함이 마땅하
며, 동사 역시 최근 공시를 통해 주주가치 제고와 주가의 저평가 인식을 드러냈다. 현
재 진행중인 40조원 자사주 매입 프로그램 종료 후 즉각적인 신규 환원 정책 도출이 
예상된다. 2027년 메모리 공급부족은 명확하다. 역사적으로 효율적 시장 내, 주가 조
정은 탄력적 복원으로 귀결되어왔다. 동사에 대한 투자의견 Buy를 유지하며, 적정주
가의 경우 320만원으로 상향한다 (26E/27E 평균 P/B 4.0배 적용). 
 Meritz Research  2026. 10. 12

Buy (20거래일 평균종가 대비 상승 여력 기준) 
적정주가 (12개월) 3,200,000원

Document 259: Allianz_2026-10-20_European defense one year on_ Money is not the problem, capacity (and delivery) are_1d7796ef

Source PDF: all report pdf/Allianz_2026-10-20_European defense one year on_ Money is not the problem, capacity (and delivery) are_1d7796ef.pdf
Report source: Allianz Trade
Date: 2026-10-20
Pages: 20
Status: ok

### Page 1

1

In Summary 
• European defense one year on: bold commitments, but is delivery keeping pace? One year 
into Europe's landmark rearmament announcements: defense spending has increased, 
financial commitments have expanded (e.g. SAFE loans, +60% commitments in 2028-2034 EU 
draft budget ) and order books have reached record highs . But industrial investment and 
delivery have yet to catch up. Defense spending growth has been cut in half  (9% vs. above 
20% in 2024/2025) , and is  set to reach  2.6% of GDP by 2030  (3.1% of GDP by 2035 ), falling 
short of the EU 2030 target of 3% and the 2035 NATO target of 3.5% of GDP . This means a 
funding gap of EUR255bn and EUR575bn, respectively.  
• In equity markets, defense companies are facing a painful reality check but the hype is not 
over yet. European defense has lost its magic (+5% YTD but -8% maximum drawdown) after 
three consecutive years of strong performance (35 -60%), a clear reality check from investors 
as countries take diverging paths : Baltic  and Nordic countries, as well as  Germany and 
Poland are leading the effort while Italy and Spain are not using the available fiscal tools to 
boost spending. Capital has been mostly channeled into  equipment, rather than long-term 
infrastructure project s (0.11% of GDP  against NATO recommendations of 1.5% of GDP). In 
term of innovation, defense is lagging the AI and robotics conversion but rising interest from 
the private investment market (above EUR10bn vs. EUR2.6bn in 202 5) suggests that the 
innovation cycle still has strong funding leeway. 
• Money is not the constraint , conversion is. Governments have poured commitments into a 
sector where newcomers cannot quickly acquire what incumbents built over decades, so 
excess demand turns into production bottlenecks and postponed projects rather than output. 
Backlogs stand at more than five times revenue while the capex ratio has stalled near 5% . 
Diverging national interests , no supranational steer  and the absence of conditionality 
mandatory clauses in public financial support give the industry little reason to invest at a time 
when higher interest rates and material costs from the Iran conflict have cut m argins (from 
10% to 8%). The shortage of skilled labor adds to the pressure as firms compete for engineers 
with civilian sectors that pay more, and cannot quickly transition their workforce into new 
complex programs. The shortage is sharpest where Europe started late : drones, space and 
cybersecurity segments account for 26%, 16% and 13% of open defense vacancies. 
• The ongoing depletion of US interceptor stocks is another warning for Europe: external  
dependence is both a security risk and a capital -efficiency problem.  To fill urgent gaps, 
Europe buys from abroad, and the import ratio has risen from 20% in 2021 to 45% in 2025. 
The US’s share in European imports has fallen from 71% to 48% but it still supplies almost half, 
and aircraft and missiles make up 82% of US purchases, with few alternatives for air defense 
and long-range missiles. We estimate it could take up to five years to rebuild some US stocks; 
until then, US domestic demand will come first, and Europe will wait in the queue behind it. 
Dependence is also expensive: Missile import prices are up 116% on the 2019 -2021 average, 
so each euro buys less capacity than budgets assume.  
• The way forward: Two horizons but one single industrial roadmap. In the short term, Europe 
needs to close the gaps in long -range missiles, air defense and cyber, and redirect idle 
automotive capacity to defense. Furthermore, it needs to back this with grants for 
infrastructure (a 0.5-1% of GDP corridor, or EUR760bn to E UR1.4trn cumulative to 2035) and 
fiscal flexibility conditional on results. In the long term, Europe needs to build supranational 
reserves and invest in drones, space, AI, robotics and quantum. These would spread benefits 
across the economy and protect the export model. Success should be judged on new capacity, 
spillovers, modernization and reliance rate, not on the share of GDP alone.  
Guillaume Dejean 
Senior Sector advisor 
guillaume.dejean@allianz -
trade.com

Ano Kuhanathan 
Head of Corporate Research

Allianz Research | 20 October 2026 
European defense one year on: Money is not the 
problem, capacity (and delivery) are

### Page 2

2

Rebuilding European defense: ambition meets reality 
One year after the Rearm Europe roadmap was unveiled, the question is no longer one of intent but means. Does 
Europe have the financial and material resources to match its rhetoric?  European defense spending is rising, but 
not fast enough. At an expected 2.3% of GDP in 2026, up from 2.2% in 2025, it is set to record a second consecutive 
year above the 2% threshold. Yet the headline masks a sharp deceleration: Annual growth in outlays is falling to 
about 9%, from more than 20% in 2024-2025, and will drift toward 4-7% by 2028-2030. This new 5-10% regime looks 
insufficient for to meet the ambitions of Rearm Europe. Based on our growth and inflation forecasts for 2026 -2027, 
and a moderate 1% growth/2% inflation path thereafter, spending is likely to climb  to only 2.6% of GDP by 2030, 
missing the 3% target. The shortfall is material: a cumulative EUR255bn funding gap over 2026 -2030, widening to 
EUR575bn if we consider NATO’s new 3.5% of GDP target for core military spending. 
Figures 1 & 2: European defense spending trajectory by 2030  / cumulative spending gap based on 3% and 3.5% 
GDP budget target (in EUR bn)

Sources: 
 European Defense Agency (EDA),  
Allianz Research  
Europe's rearmament challenge is increasingly about execution, not just ambition. At the current pace, doubling 
Europe's 2025 military equipment stock would take around five years, leaving little room for delays before the 2030 
deadline. Faster progress would require not only higher defense spending, but also a greater share of budgets 
devoted to equipment and infrastructure, alongside a stronger European industrial base. Redirecting procurement 
towards European manufacturers  by reducing the share of imports from 45% to 25% could help accelerate capacity 
expansion. Under the most optimi stic scenario, these combined measures could shorten the timeline from five to 
around three years. But Europe cannot supply itself and remains highly dependent on foreign partners to procure 
its military equipment. The defense spending allocation shows a 35-40% disbursement into new equipment (vs. 30-
35% on NATO standards)  and 0-5% into infrastructure construction/maintenance, which is quite strong by NATO 
standards but not as ambitious as promised.

Money is no longer the main constraint: capacity is 
Money is not Europe’s binding constraint . The draft 2028 -2034 EU budget lifts defense-related commitments to 
around EUR270bn, at least 60% above the roughly EUR170bn of 2021 -2027. That estimate excludes the European 
Investment Bank, which devoted 4 -5% of its annual envelope to defense in 2025 -2026, or EUR4 -5bn a year. 
Extrapolated over seven years, this adds EUR30-35bn and takes the total mark-up to about 80%. The signal matters 
as much as the amount. While national budgets face fiscal pressure, Brussels is locking in a multi-year commitment, 
with no sign of fading effort at the European level. The composition is shifting too: military funds, marginal in 2021 -
2027, become the largest block of the next envelope, a clear break with past practice. Off -budget instruments 
reinforce the message, and new tools modelled on current program s could follow if demand and delivery justify 
them. For industrials and investors, the financing architecture is in place and still expanding. The question is no 
longer whether capital exists, but whether it reaches the factories that must turn it into equ ipment.

### Page 4

SAFE loans illustrate the solution but also the problem. Off-budget and capped at EUR150bn, they should channel 
roughly EUR80bn into regional efforts if distribution is gradual and homogeneous. The reality is far from that. Only 
EUR12bn has been disbursed, about 8% of the total, against EUR131bn formally adopted. Poland, the largest 
beneficiary at EUR43.7bn, has drawn 15% of its envelope. France, Italy, Belgium and Spain have received nothing 
yet, and  Hungary’s EUR16.2bn is still pending. Industrials have long blamed red tape and lengthy validation 
procedures for construction delays and underperforming projects, and SAFE is repeating the pattern. The 
multiplicity of programs (EDIP, FED, AGILE, innovati on funds, EIB defense lines) compounds the issue: they do not 
connect to industrial segments and leave firms navigating parallel application processes. Participation is uneven as 
well, with several states slow to apply. Without faster execution, the money risks arriving after the equipment gap 
has widened, and after the political window that justified it has narrowed.  
Table 1: Safe procurement program allocation and utilization rate

Sources: 
 European Commission,  
Allianz Research

The European fiscal impulse tool was not used to its full potential. Europe’s fiscal loosening  leeway, authorizing 
18 members to run until extra deficits up to 1.5% of GDP for defense spending showed some results, but not as much 
as expected. Among positive results, it clearly helped push military spending back above NATO’s former 2%-of-GDP 
benchmark in 2025 for the first time , and keep the momentum up in  2026. Among the 18 eligible countries, 12 are 
expected to run deficits above 3% in 2026 ( 70%) and only eight of them recorded a double -digit growth of military 
spending over the 2025/2026 period compared with 2024 ahead of the launch of the tool . If some countries like 
Poland, Latvia, Estonia or even Germany are good examples of the positive externalities of the NEC on European 
defense ramp-up efforts, the overall picture is quite uneven at the European level. Indeed, a higher deficit is not the 
guarantee of stronger defense spending, as showed by  Belgium, Hungary and Slovakia , which carry high deficits 
but grew military budgets by only 4-5% in 2026. Italy, Spain and the Netherlands raised spending by a modest 5-8% 
and stayed close to Brussels’ 3% line. With interest rates high and markets volatile, France’s deficit being the 
cautionary example, that prudence is understandable. The NEC works, but it is still far from being used to its full 
potential. This clearly demonstrates that Europe is not running out of resources to rebuild its army but rather of will, 
at public or private level.

Procurement is also crowding out the long term . Spending favors short-term equipment gaps, while defense 
infrastructure stands at only 0.11% of GDP in 2026, a small share of regional investment. Quick buys beat durable 
capacity, which leaves the underlying production base no stronger. The undersubscription to SAFE loans is the 
sharpest evidence: the program did not attract enough demand, and EUR10 -20bn is likely to be reallocated to 
Ukraine. Europeans are not short of funding but of capacity to buy. Sourcing rules limit purchases outside Europe, 
yet domestic supply cannot absorb the orders either, so governments can neither buy abroad nor at home as much 
as they would like. Strict rules on foreign investment in sensitive defense assets further limit outside capital for new 
capacity, closing another route to faster expansion. The pattern points one way: Europe’s bottleneck is industrial, 
not financial. The next euro of ambition should go to factories, infrastructure and faster exe cution, not to larger 
envelopes. Otherwise Europe will keep announcing money it cannot spend.

Europe’s industrial conundrum: a record-high backlog did not translate into new investment. European defense 
has never had so much demand, yet its largest firms are not building the capacity to serve it. Order backlogs have 
hit record highs, at more than five times trailing revenue for the ten biggest listed groups, driven mostly by national 
governments. Revenue still expands at around +10% on a 12-month trailing basis, but the trend is flattening, which 
raises a question: has the peak already been reached? Capex points the same way. After the surge of 2023 -2024, 
the capex ratio has stabilized around 5%, a natural ceiling that matches the  lack of new impulse on sales. Orders 
are being booked faster than they can be converted into output. This is the industrial conundrum. The timing for 
investing could hardly be better, with full order books and political tailwinds. But two brakes are holding firms back: 
a monetization lag between orders and cash, and mild confidence in the durability of state commitment. Managers 
would rather move slowly than be left with overcapacity if budgets disappoint. The consequence is a self -inflicted 
bottleneck: the demand is there, but the supply response is rationed by caution.  
Figure 8 & 9: Revenue & investment trend of top 10 biggest defense firms in Europe* / Ammunition infrastructure 
and equipment, revenue and backlog trend in Europe**

The d efense industry is adopting a defensive approach to cash management: securing margin s rather than 
chasing new revenue . Scaling up fast without disrupting current activity or inflating costs is harder than early 
optimism suggested, and the initial enthusiasm for capacity expansion has given way to caution as delivery under 
high-stakes pressure turns out to be tougher than p lanned. Visibility is the second brake. Future budgets and 
governance remain uncertain in a shifting political landscape, with the far -right push visible in several European 
countries adding doubt over the orientation and durability of defen se priorities. All these elements put together 
drive a weak incentive to invest aggressively  in expensive and long -term project s. Market conditions have also 
turned. Tensions in the Middle East have lifted energy and raw material costs, and the S&P GSCI industrial metals 
index is back near its 2022 highs, while higher borrowing costs make every risk more expensive. The margin picture  
shows the squeeze: net profit margins of the top European defen se firms peaked around 10% in 2025 and have 
slipped back to about 8%. In this environment, protecting margins is more rational than chasing new revenue. 
Backlogs are already full and the sector enjoys relative protection from foreign competition, so there is little need 
to take risks to win new orders. Defen se groups are therefore becoming defensive themselves, prioriti zing cash 
discipline over expansion. The outcome is an industry that has the orders but not the appetite, and a Europe whose 
rearmament will be paced by corporate prudence rather than by political ambition.

The undesirable effects of a backlog. Governments have poured commitments into a sector where newcomers 
cannot acquire in a few months what incumbents built over decades, so excess demand turns into production 
bottlenecks and postponed projects rather than output. The structure compounds the problem. Defense ministries 
have become the primary customer, competition is weak and contracts are concentrated on a handful of players. 
That is the captive market trap: a secured backlog, instead of lifting investment appetite, breeds complacency and 
decision inertia on new infrastructure. Rheinmetall is the case in point. With about 60% of its backlog coming from 
the Bundeswehr, it cut its revenue guidance in Q2 after removing a naval project it had committed to but could not 
deliver as it was far from its core ammunition business. Diversification is sound only when it is coherent and gradual. 
Markets have been unforgiving: the share has lost about 35% year to date, and market capitali zation fall en to 
roughly EUR 40 -45bn while forward EBIT growth has slipped from its 2026 peak of over 50% to below 50%. The 
sector that was once in the spotlight is now paying for the hype, with disappointing delivery feeding a market verdict 
that could weigh on the next round of investment.

### Page 8

Europe’s defense industry is running out of people before it runs out of orders.  We estimate the sector employs 
650,000-700,000 people, up 30-40% since 2021, while military spending has risen 75% and backlogs have more than 
doubled. The gap is widening. In our sample of leading firms, backlog growth per point of workforce growth was 
about 2 before 2025, rose to 3 in 2025 and reache d 4 in H1 2026, as EU rearmament program s and fiscal escape 
clauses poured money into order books. This is more than a capacity limit. Firms have little room to hire at scale: 
they compete for engineers with civilian sectors that pay more, and newcomers need years of training before they 
can deliver on complex program s. Expertise cannot be bought, only built, and in defense industrial lead times are 
long and some skills exist nowhere else. The shortage is sharpest where Europe started late. Drones and UAVs 
account for 26% of open defense vacancies, space 16%, intelligence and surveillance 13%, electronics 9%: segments 
in which Europe was largely absent until recently. Industry also risks overestimating what it can absorb. Money 
moves at political speed, learning curves at industrial speed. Scaling up with too few or poorly matched staff brings 
delays and failures, so the backlog will convert slowly.  
Figures 13 & 14: backlog value vs. workforce growth for top European defense firms (2024 -H1 2026) /  Current 
open jobs in Defense sectors in Europe, per activity segment breakdown

 LSEG Datastream,  
DefenseJobsEU
 .com
, 
Allianz Research  
Who is piloting the plane ? 
Europe’s defense effort is moving at two speeds. The 2025 -2026 comparison splits the continent into leaders 
(Lithuania, Latvia, Estonia and Poland ), which are heading toward defense spending at 4.5-5.5% of GDP in 2026 
and a pack of laggards, including France, Italy, Belgium, Czechia and Slovenia, which are still below 2.5%. Denmark 
and Sweden sit in between, above the diagonal. Geography sets the gradient first: Proximity to Russia determines 
the sense of emergency. But national equipment levels and domestic industrial strength matter as well. Frontline 
states are rebuilding from thin inventories, while larger Western economies feel less pressed and can lean on 
existing stocks and industrial bases. Politics reinforces the divide. Household surveys show strong appetite for 
intervention in Sweden, Estonia, Denmark, Poland and the UK, but little in France, Germany and Italy, where 
respondents also reject trading off ot her public spending for defense. Where popular support is thin or social 
cohesion fragile, governments hesitate to push ambition. The risk is that the largest economies, which hold the 
biggest industrial bases, set the slowest pace, leaving aggregate momentum dependent on a handful of s mall, 
frontline budgets. Europe cannot be defended by its Baltic fringe alone.

Europe’s biggest competitor is itself. It is quite straightforward to see that European nations do not ride in the same 
direction. A quick look at the  industrial project pipeline shows duplication rather than scale. Two rival next -
generation fighters are under way: the Global Combat Air Program ( GCAP) led by the trident UK, Italy, Japan and 
the French-led Air Combat Fighter (ACF), the successor to former French-German Future Combat Air System (FCAS) 
initiative whose failure this year  illustrates how  national interests can sink a supra -regional target. On land, four 
separate platforms (MGCS, Challenger 3, K2PL, A2CS) are built by four different coalitions. All are not European-
led as K2PL is a hybrid partnership between Poland and South Korea to replicate the flagship K2 tank used by the 
South Korean army . This sends a diverging signal : Europe can find partners other than the US for military 
procurement, but also it still needs foreign expertise to develop heavy equipment quickly  (three-to-five year 
timeline). For comparison, some joint projects, like the French-German Main Ground Combat System (MGCS), have 
an industrial lead of over 20 years, and the latter also faces industrial divergences between partners.  Behind what 
is said about the slow development of  FCAS and MGCS is a Franco-German leadership contest that is the cause of 
all this fragmentation. While two pilots are better than none, they do not share the same view in term s of defense: 
Paris pushes sovereignty and autonomous industrial capacity, while Berlin prioriti zes rapid re -equipment to be

effective if war comes. The current air defense pipeline shows the split. The German -led Sky Shield (ESSI) targets 
2027-2030, while the European Twister interceptor, driven by France, arrives in 2035 -2040. Both logics are 
defensible, but run in parallel they fragment demand, shorten production runs and raise uni t costs. The answer is 
not black or white. Europe needs short-term equipment to be credible, and buying off the shelf abroad is sometimes 
the fastest route, as K2PL with South Korea illustrates. But overreliance on external providers has a price: a weaker 
domestic industry, lost competitiveness and strategic dependence for decades. The workable middle path is to buy 
abroad for urgent gaps while anchoring long -cycle programs in genuine cooperation, with common requirements, 
shared leadership and pooled orders. Without that, Europe will spend more and get less.  
Industrial direction won’t come from a single voice but three. France, Germany and Italy account for about 80% of 
the European revenue among the world’s top 100 arms sellers, and for 67% of the EU’s military -related output in 
2026. The two measures point to different leaders. France dominates on corporate sales (37%), while Germany leads 
on production on its territory (34%). No major project can be defined, financed or delivered without all three, which 
makes their alignment the main lever for an investment trend. The setbacks on FCAS and MGCS did more than 
delay equipment: they fed doubts about whether Europe can ramp up production to meet its targets, and markets 
and managers read those signals closely. The trio should settle priorities, share leadership by project and commit 
to joint program with fixed schedules an d pooled funding. A credible Franco -German understanding would carry 
the most weight. It would show that cohesion is possible and give industry the visibility it needs to invest. Without it, 
the efforts of smaller countries will stay fragmented, whatever their budgets.

Beyond the rivalries, Europe’s military priori ty is  clearly the sky . Long -range missiles, air and missile defen se, 
drones and modernized fighters all answer the same shift in the nature of war. Modern conflict is moving away from 
massed land and naval engagements toward air strikes and drones aimed at tactical infrastructure, where 
disruption hurts the economy as much as the army. Ukraine made the point: Drones have become full combat tools, 
used for reconnaissance and targeting as well as for dropping munitions, at a fraction of the cost of traditional 
systems. The conflict involving Iran confirmed it, with data centers and power systems among the damaged assets. 
The implication is that deterrence now depends on striking deep, intercepting cheaply and protecting critical 
networks, rather than on heavy armor or large fleets. Europe’s list of priorities reflects that lesson. The consensus on 
what to buy is real, the remaining challenge is how to organize and fund it together.

Europe needs to bet on itself, not only for sovereign reasons 
External reliance has not disappeared but is changing shape. Foreign dependence has almost doubled since 
2022, from 24% to 45% of procurement, and the US share has dropped from 71% to 48%. But Washington is still the 
main supplier, and in the UK, the Nordics and the Baltics , it still supplies the large majority of external purchases. 
What is new is that buying abroad is starting to give way to partnering. German and Israeli primes already 
cooperate on missiles, loitering munitions and satellites, and a deal to turn a German car plant into an air defense 
site is taking shape. These are early signals, but they matter. Openness is the fastest way to meet short-term demand 
when domestic capacity is stretched , and real investment commitments on European soil turn a purchase into 
capacity. But openness is also hard to square with security stakes and sovereign risk: whoever owns the technology 
and the supply chain holds a lever over Europe’s defense. The condition is close monitoring: governance, ownership, 
access to critical know-how and exit options all need to be locked in upfront. This will not be a replicable template

### Page 12

12

for the whole sector but rather a tool for targeted, specific needs where Europe cannot wait for its own industry to 
catch up. 
Figures 19 & 20: Imports of arms, non-EU share & US share among non-EU imports / US arms import share in 
Europe, per region

Data as of 2025. Sources: SIPRI, Allianz Research  
Foreign dependence is also a price problem: inefficient capital allocation due to inflationary import costs.  Since 
2022, as Europe turned to non-European suppliers for arms and ammunition, import prices per kilogram have risen 
well beyond what a technology premium can justify. Compared with the 2019-2021 average, missiles now cost 116% 
more, more than double the pre-war level. Tanks and armored vehicles are up 83%, fighters 33% and helicopters 
18%. The pattern follows dependence. Where Europe has little or no domestic alternative, as with US interceptors 
and long-range missiles, suppliers have had room to pass premium margins on to buyers facing scarcity and urgent 
demand. Higher costs linked to AI expansion and the Middle East conflict play a role, but they do not explain the 
whole picture: prices have flattened this year for most categories despite renewed energy -driven inflation, which 
points to pricing power as much as input costs. The consequence is direct. Europe pays more for the same equipment, 
not because it is worth it but because it cannot wait. Each euro committed buys less capacity than budgets assume, 
so the real size of the rearmament is smaller than the headline.

### Page 13

The US ammunition crisis: an unpleasant but welcome wake -up call. The depletion of US missile stocks is a 
warning for Europe: a single supplier can fail it at any moment, by choice or by necessity. The Iran conflict has 
consumed interceptors at a pace production cannot match. Compared with pre -war levels, our reading of the data 
shows H1 2026 inventories down about 65% for Patriot, 45% for THAAD and 30% for Tomahawk. Washington’s 
answer is a rebuild: its FY27 request targets about 3,200 Patriot interceptors and 860 THAAD interceptors. But 
factories are the bottleneck, not budgets. Backlogs at US missile makers are growing at 28% a year against revenue 
growth of 9%, and orders now stand at about three times annual sales. On current ramp-up rates, we estimate four 
years to deliver the Patriot request and five for THAAD. Until then, US domestic demand will come first, and Europe 
will wait in the queue behind it. The US is not a last -resort partner, because it has no spare capacity to lend. The 
same weapons are now in short supply on both sides of the Atlantic, so Europe cannot count on  American stocks to 
cover its own air defense.

Europe’s defense industry needs three leaders, not one 
Rebuilding defense means thinking short-term with a long-term horizon state of mind. When defining European 
priorities in terms of procurement but also industrial investment, reducing US exports of arms and ammunitions to 
Europe would help to connect the dots . Aircraft account for 63% of EU imports from the US and missiles 19%, so 
these two segments make up over four -fifths of the total. Self -reliance has to be built there first. In the short term, 
Europe should widen its sourcing to cushion the post -Iran arbitrage in US production. On missiles, the options are 
narrow: Israel is the only credible supplier outside the US, since China and Russia are not an option. That choice 
should come with local production and investment commitments. On fighters, Europe can le an on its own 
technology and speed up upgrades, which makes cutting reliance on the US realistic even in the near term. On 
drones, Ukraine offers scale and unmatched combat experience, and Europe should plug into it through joint 
production and shared prac tice. Türkiye can support local manufacturing, and South Korea can help on armored 
vehicles and robotics as well.

Defense capacity should not be exclusively assessed in hardware . Software, IT and telecoms are overlooked, 
though new equipment carries a heavy digital load and produces vast amounts of data. Used well, that data is a 
performance driver. Used poorly, it is a vulnerability. Cybersecurity is the other missing layer: mod ern conflict is 
fought on networks as much as on the ground. It is no accident that, in the extra funding requested by the US after 
the Iran clash, cybersecurity stands at about 8% and is the largest dedicated capability line after munitions. Europe 
should match that signal. There is also a risk of new dependence on the US here. Leading AI models are ahead in 
detecting system flaws and patching them, and relying on them would hand a foreign provider a view into critical 
European systems. Europe should invest in open-weight models it can run, audit and adapt on its own infrastructure, 
and pair them with a European cyber shield. This is an under -funded segment and a strategic one: capacity that 
cannot be switched off from abroad.

A two-horizon roadmap 
Horizon 1 - Filling the short-term hole to secure protection minimum 
The most important action is being consistent. On the current path, spending reaches about 3.1% of GDP by 2035, 
close to EUR800bn a year. For a region that was below 2% only a few years ago, that is a strong result. The 3% 
objective may have been set too early, at a time when industry could not absorb the funds. The 3.5% and 5% paths 
show the stakes: they would require roughly EUR900bn and EUR1.3trn a year by 2035, volumes that factories and 
supply chains could not deliver on today’s footing. Policy should therefore be judged on more than one number. 
Four measures matter more. First, new output capacity: how many more missiles, vehicles and munitions Europe 
can produce each year. Second, industrial externalities for automotive, electronics, software and materials. Third, 
modernization: whether equipment and technology are up to date and are competitive for export market. Fourth, 
the reliance rate: the share of capability that depends on non -European suppliers. A budget that rises while these 
four indicators stagnate is a failure. A smaller one that raises  capacity and lowers dependence is a success. Europe 
should publish them alongside the spending ratio. 
Figure 27: EU-27 defense spending as % of GDP trajectory over 2026-2035 period

Sources: European Commission,  
European Defense Agency,  
Allianz Research  
Build the ground before the factories. Europe has bought the missiles and forgotten the ground they stand on. 
Infrastructure is where rearmament stalls: production sites, depots and logistics do not build themselves, and a 
sleepy private investment cycle will not wake without a push. Government s should provide it, through grants and 
subsidies aimed at defense infrastructure. NATO’s 1.5% of GDP line by 2035 should be read as a compass, not a 
deadline. Reaching it would mean covering a hole above EUR2trn, on top of procurement bills that are alrea dy 
heavy. Europe should set a realistic corridor of 0.5-1% instead, which still demands EUR760bn to EUR1.4trn of effort. 
Public money cannot replace the market, and it should not try. Its job is narrower: lower the entry cost, signal 
permanence and rekindle an investment engine slowed by high rates. Every euro of support should buy a 
commitment in return, on capacity, deadlines and European location. Defense becomes a new vertical of European 
industrial policy, planned with the ambition once reserved for energy and chips.

No free lunch: look at home before buying abroad . Europe’s car plants run below potential while its defen se lines 
run beyond it. This is an opportunity for European governments to play matchmaker and build up industrial bridges, 
especially as the automotive industry slows down . S teering idle automotive capacity, workforce and supplier 
networks toward defense primes in a first step, then lifting output without burning scarce capital would kill two birds 
with one stone: loosening  bottlenecks in defen se while easing the production cost structure for automakers . Each 
new link proves the two industrial agendas can be synchronized. Procurement must follow the same logic. Domestic 
orders come with capex commitments attached. Foreign technology is welcome only when the need is critical and 
recurrent, and only if it brings production and investment onto European soil. Buying abroad with no local return is 
a bill, not a strategy. The short -term priorities are narrow and clear: long -range missiles, air defen se to shield 
infrastructure and cyber tools against disinformation and external attacks. Europe should treat price as a security 
variable: build competing sources, pool orders to gain bargaining power and tie large contracts to production on 
European soil.

Flexibility is earned, not granted. The fiscal impulse is fading while rates are getting higher and stagflation risks in 
Europe are rising, which means public effort could weaken just when momentum is needed. Greater fiscal flexibility 
is required but  an extension of the national escape clause (NEC) is perhaps not the right answer to underpin a 
constant and robust investment cycle as results so far have been quite mixed. Incentives are missing and here is the 
crux of the capacity conundrum in Europe. Instead, a double approach in which a big military spender – either the 
state or a corporate - would be rewarded by a fiscal loosening measure and easier credit access  but support would 
be this time conditional and monitored against tangible results. Spending above budget lines would be authorized 
if, and only if, it is channeled into European-based industrial projects for identified critical equipment or resources , 
and key milestones are met on time. For firms, a tax-rebate of 1 to 2% could be applied in case of strong investment 
commitment that would be evaluate in function of their current capex polic ies and backlog level s. This should 
reassure sceptics who see another loosening of the rules as a loophole, and give markets what they want to see: 
ambition with discipline. Used well, a conditional waiver turns fiscal rules from an obstacle into an instrument of 
industrial strategy. Used badly, it becomes Europe’s next debt debate.

Horizon 2: Thinking on a 2030 defensive line 
Building supranational reserves as the ultimate disruption-mitigation tool. Let’s take US as example. Having the 
biggest and most performing defense industry does not prevent it from building up strategic reserves to anticipate 
market shocks and/or a wartime-led demand surge. Europe should do the same for critical equipment, arms and 
ammunition. A reserve cuts dependence on foreign supply and buys time when conflict closes borders and ports. It 
also needs ownership beyond national flags. Build supranational governance a nd a shared financing base, with 
European borrowing on the table, so that every capital feels concerned. Condition ing financial support on joint 
procurement channels  would end the national conflicts of interest that have sunk projects before. The ideal is a  
nation-free fleet of arms and ammunition, usable only under NATO governance , with clear intermediate targets so 
that private firms can plan capacity, hire and invest with confidence.

Modernize or be dependent again... Modernization is the best protection against new dependence, and it protects 
what Europe sells best: defense exports are a major revenue line, and they erode the moment the continent slips off 
the technology curve. The priorities are clear. First, build an air -defense and drone industry at scale. Second, widen 
the footprint in space, with broader constellations and stronger launch capacity. Third, invest in AI, the most under -
funded defense segment, and fuse it with the cyber shield. Automation and robotics are the multiplier. Autonomous 
mobility advances from the car industry can transfer, and R&D should be pooled across sectors. Joint investment 
with semiconductor, software and base -metal producers should lift material quality, onboard intelligence and 
computing power. Quantum belongs in the same pipeline. The primary goal is a leading-edge European model that 
can be exported, not an assembly of imported parts.  
…and lose your best trade engine and political stabilizer . For years, aerospace and aircraft have been Europe's 
most performing segment in trade exchanges, generating a consistent EUR30–35bn annual surplus toward non-EU 
members. More recently, the ramp -up in defense capacity has added EUR1–3bn per year in weapons and 
ammunition exports, a contribution that is far from negligible. But this is not just about trade balances. European 
defense expertise is widely acknowledged abroad and serves as the most effective diplomatic connector during 
high-level political engagements with international partners. It is, in essence, a powerful geopolitical tool: defense 
equipment and know -how open doors to broader bilateral agreements, strengthen alliances and help counter 
global fragmentation. Failing to inves t in military modernization does not just weaken Europe's security – it risks 
dismantling the very engine that fuels its trade influence and strategic relevance on the world stage.

Drones and space are the bright spot, AI and robotics are under-invested. Europe’s defense technology is being 
rebuilt, but so far capital transmission has mostly being channeled where Europe was the weakest and political will 
was strongest: drones collected near half of defense -related investment in the European venture capital market 
(44% since 2020) , and space and satellite imagery follow. In the meantime, AI-for-defense has attracted almost 
nothing, and robotics only a fraction of drone funding. Yet warfare is shifting toward digital and  electric-powered 
assets. The economy runs on algorithms, data centers and power grids, which makes them primary targets, as this 
year’s Middle East conflict showed .  In this context, European governments and defense primes should (1) treat AI 
as a strategic asset and open procurement to outside technology firms; (2) use co -investment and guaranteed 
demand to channel private capital into robotics and autonomy, which should also ease the sector’s labor shortages, 
and (3) protect data centers and grids as defense infrastructure. The US and China are investing heavily in both 
fields and could turn that lead into a battlefield edge. Europe should not repeat in AI the delay it has just started to 
correct in drones.

these factors may be more likely to occur, or more pronounced, as a result of terrorist activities and their 
consequences.

Điểm nhấn thị trường 
Chỉ số, nhóm ngành, khối ngoại và phái sinh

Top 5 ngành tăng / giảm  VNINDEX giảm điểm trong tuần qua, -30.55 điểm tương ứng -1.68%, với thanh khoản suy 
giảm. Dệt may, Tiện ích khí và Bao bì & đóng gói là các nhóm ngành có mức giảm rõ rệt 
nhất trong tuần. Khối ngoại đảo chiều bán ròng so với xu hướng của tuần trước với giá trị  
-2,865 tỷ đồng, tập trung ở các mã VHM, VPB và VIC; đồng thời, tự doanh đảo chiều bán 
ròng ở mức -146 tỷ đồng.

Ở một khía cạnh khác, khối ngoại bán ròng -3,351 HĐTL VN30F1M trong tuần qua nhưng 
đang duy trì vị thế mua lũy kế 1,650 hợp đồng. Tự doanh mua ròng 2,822 hợp đồng nhưng 
đang duy trì trạng thái lũy kế bán ròng -6,636 hợp đồng.

TTCK Việt Nam bước vào tuần đầu tiên ở vị thế thị trường mới nổi bằng một nhịp điều 
chỉnh, khi VN-Index để mất mốc 1.800 điểm và lùi về đóng cửa quanh 1.785 điểm. Đây là 
phản ứng "mua kỳ vọng, bán thực tế" khá điển hình: ngay trong phiên nâng hạng chính 
thức 21/9, chỉ số mở cửa hưng phấn rồi nhanh chóng đảo chiều do áp lực chốt lời, với 
nhóm cổ phiếu Vingroup (VIC, VHM, VRE) giảm mạnh dẫn dắt đà đi xuống, và sức ép lan 
rộng sang bất động sản, ngân hàng, năng lượng về cuối tuần. Điểm đáng chú ý là dòng 
vốn ngoại đã tiếp tục bán ròng ngay sau cột mốc ngày 21/9, dù đã mua ròng trở lại trong 
tuần trước đó. Bối cảnh bên ngoài cũng kém thuận lợi khi Fed vừa tăng lãi suất và phát đi 
tín hiệu diều hâu, chứng khoán Mỹ điều chỉnh, giá dầu neo cao, và lợi suất trái phiếu 
Chính phủ Mỹ tăng vọt. Trong một, hai tuần tới, bên cạnh các yếu tố ngoại biên, mùa báo 
cáo KQKD quý 3 cũng sẽ bắt đầu tác động lên diễn biến thị trường chung.

Lợi nhuận sau thuế công ty mẹ tăng 
trưởng khả quan trong 2Q26 14% 
YoY, với động lực chính đến từ mảng 
dịch vụ CNTT nước ngoài

Mảng CNTT nước ngoài: Lĩnh vực dịch 
vụ IT phân cực mạnh mẽ do tác nhân 
AI (AI Agentic) s ẽ củng c ố xu hư ớng 
đầu tư vào AI

FPT ở vị thế thuận lợi cho phép 
doanh nghiệp đa dạng hóa công cụ 
AI và tối ưu chi phí vận hành

Khuyến ngh ị MUA với giá m ục tiêu 
86,000 VND/cổ phiếu 
 Do không còn h ạch toán FPT Telecom (FOX) là công ty con, d oanh thu 2Q2026 của 
FPT giảm mạnh còn 13,810 tỷ VND (-17% YoY). Lợi nhuận sau thuế công ty mẹ không 
bị ảnh hưởng bởi thay đổi hạch toán đạt 2,568 tỷ VND (14% YoY). Mảng dịch vụ CNTT 
tại thị trường nước ngoài tiếp tục là đ ộng lực tăng trư ởng chính, đạt doanh thu và 
LNTT lần lượt là 9,868/1,538 tỷ VND (16.3% /19.3% YoY).

Sự chuyển dịch chi tiêu từ dịch vụ IT truyền thống sang chuyển đổi số và tích hợp AI 
được kỳ vọng sẽ diễn ra ngày m ột mạnh mẽ khi: i) Sự phát triển của năng l ực tính 
toán giúp kéo giảm chi phí token (đơn v ị năng lực tính toán của AI) và thúc đ ẩy nhu 
cầu sử dụng AI của doanh nghiệp; ii) Các mô hình AI mã nguồn mở gia tăng lựa chọn 
cho người dùng sẽ thúc đẩy chi tiêu

Đối với các doanh nghiệp ứng dụng sâu các mô hình AI phục vụ hoạt động kinh doanh 
như FPT , chúng tôi nhận thấy xu hướng tối ưu chi phí tốt hơn nhờ khả năng sử dụng 
các mô hình AI đ ến từ đa dạng nhà cung c ấp; với chi phí trên hi ệu năng tính toán 
được cải thi ện. Trong k ịch b ản cơ s ở, chúng tôi d ự báo biên l ợi nhuận trước thu ế 
mảng CNTT nước ngoài sẽ ổn định ở mức 15.4% nhờ duy trì chi ến lược quản trị chi 
phí hiệu quả qua đó giúp LNTT 2026/2027 đạt 6,198/7,087 tỷ VND (13.3%/14.3% YoY).

Dựa trên định giá FCFF và P/E, triển vọng kinh doanh cũng như xem xét các rủi ro có 
thể phát sinh, chúng tôi khuyến nghị MUA đối với cổ phiếu FPT . Giá mục tiêu là 86,000 
VND/cổ phiếu.

Mảng cho thuê văn phòng đứng trước 
cơ hội lớn đóng góp vào tăng trư ởng 
nhờ các điều kiện thuận lợi

Khuyến nghị MUA với giá mục tiêu 
58,100 VNĐ/cổ phiếu

Doanh thu Quý 2/2026 giảm nhẹ, đạt 2,432 tỷ VND (-3% YoY/-2% QoQ), trong khi l ợi 
nhuận sau thuế công ty mẹ đạt 632 tỷ VND (+1% YoY/-11.5% QoQ), do doanh thu của 
mảng bất động sản suy giảm. Qua đó, REE hoàn thành lần lượt 40%/47.8% kế hoạch 
doanh thu và LNST công ty mẹ 2026.

Trong Q2.2026, tổng sản lượng phát đi ện của REE tăng nh ẹ, đạt 2.8 tỷ kWh (+2.3% 
YoY) với đóng góp tăng trưởng rõ nét đến từ mảng điện gió khi dự án Duyên Hải V1-4 
bắt đầu vận hành; tuy nhiên  sản lượng thủy điện suy gi ảm do tác đ ộng tiêu c ực từ 
pha thời tiết El Nino, đạt 1.1 tỷ kWh.

Với năng lực chuyên môn được nâng cao, mảng M&E ghi nhận những dấu hiệu phục 
hồi tích cực trong giá trị hợp đồng ký mới trong 1H.2026, 5,865 tỷ VND (cao hơn 53.9% 
so với giá trị ký mới cả năm 2025). KBSV dự phóng doanh thu c ủa mảng M&E sẽ đạt 
4,499/5,190 tỷ VND (+28%/15% YoY) trong 2026/2027.

Hội tụ bởi 3 yếu tố: i) Định hướng phát triển kinh tế Hồ Chí Minh trở thành trung tâm 
tài chính quốc tế; ii) Hạ tầng giao thông ngày một được hoàn thiện và iii) Chi phí thuê 
cạnh tranh so với chất lượng dịch vụ của E. Town 6, KBSV dự phóng doanh thu mảng 
cho thuê văn phòng trong 2026/2027 tăng trưởng +14%/6.2% YoY.

Dựa trên đ ịnh giá SOTP , triển vọng kinh doanh cũng như xem xét các r ủi ro có th ể 
phát sinh, chúng tôi khuy ến nghị MUA đối với cổ phiếu REE. Giá m ục tiêu là 58,100 
VND/cổ phiếu, cao hơn 28.3% so với mức giá đóng cửa tại ngày 15/09/2026.

Doanh thu 2Q.26 bứt phá nhờ khoản 
thu nhập bất thường của nhiệt điện 
Cà Mau và Vũng Áng

Nhiệt đi ện than duy trì công su ất ở 
mức cao nh ờ nguồn cung nhiên li ệu 
ổn định trong 2H26

Điện khí Đông Nam Bộ (NT1&2 và 
NT3&4) có triển vọng hoạt động 
thuận lợi

Khuyến nghị MUA với giá mục tiêu 
18,100 VND/cổ phiếu 
 Trong Quý 2/2026, POW ghi nhận Doanh thu và LNST cổ đông công ty mẹ đạt lần lượt 
20,308 tỷ VND (116.1% YoY) và 3,519 tỷ VND (484.6% YoY) chủ yếu nhờ các khoản thu 
nhập từ chênh lệch tiền khí của nhiệt điện Cà Mau và tỷ giá của nhiệt điện Vũng Áng. 
Với kết qu ả trên, POW hoàn thành l ần lư ợt 65%/420% kế hoạch doanh thu và l ợi 
nhuận sau thuế công ty mẹ.

Chúng tôi cho r ằng nhà máy nhi ệt than Vũng Áng trong 2026 s ẽ được duy trì công 
suất huy động ở mức cao nhờ: i) Xác suất diễn ra El Nino tăng cao và dự kiến kéo dài 
đến đầu 2027, ii) Nguồn cung than trong nước được bảo đảm từ các đối tác (TKV, TCT 
Đông B ắc) và d ự trữ than d ồi dào c ủa doanh nghi ệp ghi nh ận ở mức cao trong 
Q2/2026.

Dự án đường dây 500kV Mạch 3 đi vào vận hành giúp giảm áp lực cạnh tranh của các 
nhà máy điện tại khu vực phía Nam, qua đó kỳ vọng doanh thu của NT1&2 trong năm 
2026 đạt 11,104 tỷ VND (5.3% YoY). Đối với dự án điện NT3&4, khoản thu nhập chênh 
lệch sản lượng theo giá công suất do sản lượng điện thực tế thấp hơn so với tỷ lệ Qc 
65% của nhà máy được duy trì sang 2H26 và 2027 sẽ giúp cho dự án ghi nhận doanh 
thu lần lượt 17,848/17,621 tỷ VND doanh thu trong 2026/2027.

tiêu là 18,100 VND/cổ phiếu.

CTCP Dịch vụ Hàng không Taseco (AST) 
Tiếp tục mở rộng điểm kinh doanh 
22/09/2026  
Chuyên viên cao cấp Nguyễn Ngọc Anh 
(+84) 24-7303-5333 anhntn@kbsec.com.vn

KQKD tăng trư ởng tích c ực trong 
2Q2026

Tăng trư ởng khách qua c ảng hàng 
không thúc đ ẩy doanh thu t ại các 
điểm kinh doanh hiện hữu

Mở rộng mạng lưới tạo động lực tăng 
trưởng doanh thu mới

Cơ c ấu kinh doanh và hi ệu qu ả vận 
hành hỗ trợ cải thiện biên lợi nhuận

Chúng tôi khuyến nghị MUA, giá mục 
tiêu 76,500 VND/cổ phiếu

2Q2026, AST ghi nh ận doanh thu và LNST đ ạt 479/92 t ỷ VND (+22%/19% yoy), với 
động lực chủ yếu đến từ (1) đóng góp từ 19 điểm kinh doanh mở mới tại T3 Tân Sơn 
Nhất và (2) tăng trưởng tích cực của các mảng hàng miễn thuế, nhà hàng - fastfood 
và phòng ch ờ thương gia. Lũy k ế 6T2026, doanh thu và LNST đ ạt 1,035/221 t ỷ VND 
(+33%/46% yoy), lần lượt hoàn thành 48%/49% kế hoạch năm.

Chúng tôi dự phóng lượng khách quốc tế/nội địa tại các thị trường AST khai thác tăng 
bình quân 11%/5% mỗi năm đến 2030. Lưu lượng và tỷ trọng khách quốc tế gia tăng 
được kỳ vọng thúc đẩy số giao dịch và doanh thu bình quân trên mỗi khách, đặc biệt 
tại mảng miễn thuế, bán lẻ và phòng chờ.

Chúng tôi dự phóng số điểm kinh doanh AST tăng từ 145 điểm cuối năm 2026 lên 182 
điểm năm 2030. Sau đóng góp t ừ 19 điểm tại T3 Tân Sơn Nh ất và 6 điểm mới tại T2 
Nội Bài từ 2025 - 2026, các dự án Long Thành, Phú Qu ốc và Gia Bình ti ếp tục mở ra 
dư địa mở rộng mạng lưới trong trung và dài hạn cho doanh nghiệp.

Việc mở rộng các hoạt động mang lại lợi nhuận cao, bao gồm hàng miễn thuế, phòng 
chờ thương gia và tối ưu hoạt động tại các điểm kinh doanh đ ược kỳ vọng tác động 
tích c ực lên biên l ợi nhu ận của AST . Chúng tôi dự phóng biên l ợi nhu ận g ộp đ ạt 
64.7%/65.5% trong năm 2026/2027, hỗ trợ LNST công ty mẹ tăng lần lượt 48%/17% 
yoy.

Chúng tôi khuy ến nghị MUA đối với AST , giá mục tiêu 76 ,500 VND/cổ phiếu, tương 
đương tiềm năng tăng giá 20% so với giá đóng cửa ngày 21/09/2026.

Doanh thu 2Q2026 giảm nhẹ do 
không còn ghi nhận doanh thu 1 lần 
từ phí nước thải; trong khi LNST 
công ty mẹ tăng trưởng 1 chữ số

Tiêu thụ nước khu vực dân sinh đư ợc 
thúc đẩy nhờ thời tiết nắng nóng hơn; 
trong khi tri ển v ọng nhóm khách 
hàng công nghi ệp b ị ảnh hư ởng b ởi 
bất lợi thuế quan

Đơn giá thu gom, vận chuyện và xử 
lý rác thải được phê duyệt cho phép 
cải thiện doanh thu và lợi nhuận của 
mảng này

Khuyến nghị MUA với giá mục tiêu 
59,000 VNĐ/cổ phiếu

Doanh thu 2Q2026 đạt 1,222 tỷ (-8% YoY/18.1% QoQ) và LNST công ty mẹ đạt 325 tỷ 
VND (7% YoY/55% QoQ), ghi nhận tăng trưởng của tất cả các mảng. Mảng cung cấp 
nước tăng trưởng ổn định, tiếp tục là trụ cột chính của BWE, đóng góp doanh thu 785 
tỷ VND (+12% YoY/12% QoQ) nhờ nhà máy nước Long An v ận hành từ Q3/2025 giúp 
sản lượng nước tăng 28% YoY.

Cường độ và thời gian kéo dài c ủa các đ ợt nắng nóng trong 2026/2027 là cơ s ở để 
chúng tôi đi ều chỉnh tăng tăng trư ởng tiêu th ụ nước của nhóm khách hàng dân cư 
tại Bình Dương và Bình Phư ớc trong năm 2026 - 2027 lên mức 7.5/6.5% YoY. Trái lại, 
triển vọng tăng trưởng tiêu thụ nước nhóm sản xuất ảm đạm do tình hình thuế quan 
có diễn biến bất lợi trong ngắn hạn cho các ngành xu ất khẩu truyền thống của Việt 
Nam.

UBND TP .HCM công bố đơn giá thu gom, vận chuyển và xử lý rác thải mới, tăng mạnh 
từ 50-150% nhằm phản ánh đầy đủ và chính xác hơn các chi phí phát sinh trong thực 
tế. Chúng tôi cho rằng điều này sẽ tác động tích cực với BWE khi các dự án của doanh 
nghiệp ở mảng này s ẽ vận hành hi ệu quả hơn. Dự báo doanh thu và l ợi nhuận gộp 
của mảng xử lý rác thải lần lượt đạt 1,069/1,234 và 165.7/180.7 tỷ VND lần lượt trong 
2026/2027, tương ứng mức tăng 34%/15% và 27%/9% YoY.

Kết hợp 2 phương pháp định giá FCFF và EV/EBITDA, KBSV khuyến nghị MUA với BWE, 
giá mục tiêu 59,000 VND, tương đương với mức lợi suất kỳ vọng 42.2% so với giá đóng 
cửa ngày 22/09/2026.

Ngưỡng kỹ thuật

VNINDEX điều chỉnh trở lại trong tuần qua sau khi gặp thử thách tại vùng cản quanh 1850, 
hình thành cây nến tuần giảm điểm nhưng với phần bóng dưới khá dài cho thấy sự tham 
gia tích cực của lực cầu bắt đáy tại vùng hỗ trợ gần. Trên khung ngày, nhịp giảm mạnh giữa 
tuần đã khiến chỉ số đánh mất đáy ngắn hạn tuần trước và chớm cắt xuống dưới SMA200, 
trong khi phiên hồi phục cuối tuần chưa cho thấy sự cải thiện rõ rệt của lực cầu. Mặc dù 
diễn biến này cho thấy áp lực điều chỉnh vẫn có thể còn tiếp diễn, tuy nhiên khi xu hướng 
tăng ngắn hạn chưa bị phá vỡ, VNINDEX được kỳ vọng sẽ nhận được lực đỡ tại vùng hỗ trợ 
gần quanh 1750, tương ứng vùng hợp lưu quanh SMA50, và có cơ hội hồi phục trở lại. 
Kháng cự xa: 1880 (+-15) 
Kháng cự gần: 1830 (+-10) 
Hỗ trợ gần: 1750 (+-10) 
Hỗ trợ xa: 1710 (+-15)

Danh mục đầu tư mẫu

Phương pháp tiếp cận: 
- Danh mục tập hợp các cổ phiếu tiềm năng nhất được KBSV 
lựa chọn và tái cơ cấu theo tháng. 
- Ngưỡng cắt lỗ cần được cân nhắc cho mỗi mã là -15%. 
- Danh mục giả định luôn duy trì tỷ trọng 100% là cổ phiếu với 
trọng số được phân bổ đều giữa các mã.

Lưu ý:  
- NĐT được khuyến nghị mua tại mức giá bằng hoặc thấp hơn 
giá khuyến nghị mua. 
- Lợi nhuận kỳ vọng được tính theo giá khuyến nghị mua. 
 So sánh hiệu suất với VN30 Index

Document 326: FOMO's Rollercoaster Versus FEMO's Steady Climb

Source PDF: all report pdf/FOMO's Rollercoaster Versus FEMO's Steady Climb.pdf
Report source: researchmail轉寄
Date: 2026-10-20
Pages: 10
Status: ok

### Page 1

From:    Yardeni QuickTakes <yardeni-research@ghost.io>
Subject: FOMO's Rollercoaster Versus  FEMO's Steady Climb
Date:    5 hours ago
FOMO's Rollercoaster Versus FEMO's Steady Climb

YARDENI QUICKTAKES
FOMO's Rollercoaster Versus
FEMO's Steady Climb
By Ed Yardeni & Elias Griepentrog • 19 Oct 2026
View in browser

### Page 2

Photo by Jonny Gios / Unsplash
I. FOMO vs FEMO
Forecasting the stock market is easy! Just multiply the P/E (price-to-
earnings ratio) by E (earnings per share) to get P (the stock price index).
The hard part, of course, is getting the P/E and E right. We call the P/E
component FOMO (fear of missing out) and the E component FEMO
(fabulous earnings momentum). This year, investors have been on a
rollercoaster ride as FOMO has fluctuated mostly to the downside, while

FEMO has continued climbing.

### Page 3

More recently, concerns about AI investment returns and rising US
Treasury yields have tightened credit conditions, raising fears about the
sustainability of the AI investment boom and weighing on P/Es. Since
early October, multiple compression has outpaced continued earnings
gains, dragging many stocks lower.
Semiconductors have been at the center of the recent rout, with the
semiconductor stock price index plunging 15.7% over eight consecutive
sessions. However, FOMO staged a comeback today, with the Nasdaq

rising 1.1% and semiconductors gaining 1.6%, snapping their losing
streak. The FOMO rollercoaster continues, but FEMO remains firmly on
an upward trajectory.
Here is more:
(1) S&P 500. So far this year, forward earnings surged 31.0% ytd through
October 19, more than offsetting a 14.2% contraction in the forward P/E.
Consequently, the index is up 12.3% ytd (chart).
(2) Information Technology. The divergence is even more striking in the
S&P 500 Information Technology sector. Forward earnings has soared

65.5% ytd, while the forward P/E has contracted 25.6%, leaving the
sector's stock price index up 23.0% ytd (chart). Since early October,
however, falling P/Es have outpaced rising earnings, pulling IT stock
prices lower.

### Page 4

(3) Semiconductors. FEMO is incredibly strong in the S&P 500
Semiconductors industry, which now accounts for a record 55.0% of the IT
sector's forward earnings and 40.9% of its market capitalization (chart).
That implies a significant forward P/E discount to the rest of the IT sector
and to the S&P 500. The discount reflects concerns that rapidly rising
borrowing costs for highly leveraged AI infrastructure providers could slow
the AI investment boom and weaken demand for chips. Semiconductor

earnings continue to soar, but that's because capacity constraints are
boosting semiconductor prices, which should fall sooner or later as either
demand slows or capacity expands, or both.
(4) Healthy constraints. We view investors' growing concerns about the
AI boom as good news. Financial, social, political, and physical
constraints should reduce overinvestment risks and keep the boom on a
sustainable path. Recently, financial constraints have been in focus, with

### Page 5

CoreWeave's bond yields surging above 15% last week as the Bond
Vigilantes increasingly scrutinized AI borrowers. The bond market is
playing a welcome role in allocating capital more efficiently. Meanwhile,
the big hyperscalers remain the boom's main drivers, supported by strong
cash flows and ample access to capital. The AI rollercoaster may be
hitting speed bumps, but that will help keep it on a steadier path.
II. Industrial Production
The strength of the US economy continues to underpin strong earnings

growth. Industrial production rose 0.7% m/m in September, its biggest
gain since April, and 2.2% y/y, the strongest since September 2022
(charts). Leading the way higher was utilities output. That reflects rising
electricity demand from data centers and manufacturing, as well as
weather-related demand. Manufacturing output edged up 0.3%.

### Page 6

Since ChatGPT was released in late 2022, technology-related industrial
production has risen sharply to record highs (chart).
Defense and space equipment production also continues to increase at a
solid pace, supported by rising military spending and the rapid growth of
the space industry (chart).

### Page 7

III. Federal Deficit
Fiscal policy also continues to boost economic growth, though at the cost
of large federal deficits and mounting federal debt. The federal budget
deficit totaled $1.99 trillion over the 12 months through September, while
marketable Treasury debt increased by $2.12 trillion (chart).
Federal outlays reached $7.40 trillion, including $3.77 trillion for health
care, Medicare, and Social Security (chart). Notably, net interest payments

reached a record $1.05 trillion, exceeding defense spending of $970
billion.

### Page 8

Tax receipts totaled $5.40 trillion, supported by rising individual income
and payroll taxes as well as tariff revenues (chart). Corporate income tax
receipts have been depressed this year by the 100% depreciation
allowance granted by last year's One Big Beautiful Bill Act (OBBB).
IV. Foreign Capital Flows
Given Corporate America's FEMO, it is not surprising that foreign capital
continues to pour into US financial markets. Over the 12 months through

August, net foreign purchases of US equities totaled a whopping $1.01
trillion, followed by corporate bonds and Treasury notes and bonds (chart).
Apparently, foreign investors have a greater appetite for US equities and
corporate bonds than for US Treasuries.

### Page 9

Net foreign equity inflows surged to a record high, far exceeding previous
peaks (chart). Private investors accounted for $844 billion, while foreign
official institutions contributed another $165 billion, a remarkable
development given their historically limited participation in US equity
markets.
Foreigners purchased more US corporate bonds than US Treasury notes
and bonds over the past 12 months (chart).

### Page 10

clause.

Document 391: Sustainable bank bond supply set for further growth in 2027

Source PDF: all report pdf/Sustainable bank bond supply set for further growth in 2027.pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 10
Status: ok

### Page 1

Sustainable bank bond supply is close to reaching a record in
2026, despite Europe’s ESG simpliﬁcation agenda. We expect
another increase to €100bn in 2027, supported by sustainable
lending growth, higher redemptions and resilient investor
demand
Maureen Schuller
Sustainable bank bond supply is blooming, nearing a record in 2026
Banks issued an unexpectedly large amount of sustainable debt
this year despite Europe’s ESG deregulation eﬀorts. Up to mid-

October 2026, they issued more than €86bn in sustainable bonds
across diﬀerent instrument types, up by almost €20bn from the
same period last year.
This was a surprisingly strong increase given that favourable
primary market conditions oﬀered issuers little greenium, despite
continued evidence that sustainable bonds attract higher investor
demand, as reﬂected in stronger oversubscription rates. This
suggests that the availability of sustainable assets was not a

### Page 2

meaningful constraint on banks' sustainable debt issuance, thanks
to higher redemptions and sustainable lending growth.
Issuers are also increasingly directing ESG bond issuance towards
European capital markets, often at the expense of North American
markets, reﬂecting the growing divergence in political and
regulatory priorities between the two regions.
The bulk of the YTD supply (€76.6bn) (re)ﬁnanced green assets, with
green supply up €25bn from 2025. Instead, issuance in social and

sustainability formats did not exceed €9.6bn, trailing last year’s
supply by €4.5bn. All instrument types, from secured to unsecured
to subordinated, contributed to the higher ESG supply volumes.
However, sustainable T2 and bail-in senior (senior non-preferred
and HoldCo) issuance increased particularly strongly, as the
funding cost advantage from sustainable issuance was better for
these instruments than for covered bonds and preferred senior
debt.

Issuance under the European Green Bond Standard (EuGBS) rose
from €2.5bn to almost €12bn YTD. For many banks, ICMA-aligned
green bond issuance remained the more practical option as it
allows them to ﬁnance a broader pool of assets, rather than fully
commit the bond proceeds to Taxonomy-aligned activities. This is
particularly relevant when non-EU assets are ﬁnanced, for which
the EU regulation-based do-no-signiﬁcant-harm requirements
(DNSH) are diﬃcult to apply.

That said, with the revisions to the EU Taxonomy’s technical
screening criteria around the corner (expected in 4Q 2026), we
expect more banks to inaugurate European Green Bond
Programmes, as more types of green assets will become available
due to the EU’s simpliﬁcation agenda.
